A Fourier–Jacobi Dirichlet series for cusp forms on orthogonal groups
Authors: Rafail Psyroukis
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
We investigate a Dirichlet series involving the Fourier–Jacobi coefficients of two cusp forms F, G for orthogonal groups of signature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2,n+2)$$\end{document}(2,n+2). In the case when F is a Hecke eigenform and G is a Maass lift of a Poincaré series, we establish a connection with the standard L-function attached to F. What is more, we find explicit choices of orthogonal groups, for which we obtain a clear-cut Euler product expression for this Dirichlet series. Through our considerations, we recover a classical result for Siegel modular forms, first introduced by Kohnen and Skoruppa, but also provide a range of new examples, which can be related to other kinds of modular forms, such as paramodular, Hermitian, and quaternionic.

Introduction

Kohnen and Skoruppa in [13] considered the Dirichlet series\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}_{F,G}(s) = \zeta (2s-2k+4)\sum _{N=1}^{\infty } \langle \phi _N, \psi _N \rangle N^{-s}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DF,G(s)=ζ(2s-2k+4)∑N=1∞⟨ϕN,ψN⟩N-s,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _N, \psi _N$$\end{document}ϕN,ψN are the Nth Fourier-Jacobi coefficients of two genus two Siegel cusp forms F, G of integral weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 0$$\end{document}k≥0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \;, \;\rangle $$\end{document}⟨,⟩ denotes the Petersson inner product on the space of Jacobi forms of index N and weight k. They showed that this Dirichlet series has an analytic continuation to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C and satisfies a functional equation. Moreover, in the case when F is a Hecke eigenform and G is in the Maass space, they deduced that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}_{F,G}(s) = \langle \phi _1, \psi _1\rangle Z_{F}(s), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DF,G(s)=⟨ϕ1,ψ1⟩ZF(s),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{F}(s)$$\end{document}ZF(s) is the spinor L-function attached to F. In the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _1$$\end{document}ϕ1 does not vanish identically, this gives a way of proving the meromorphic continuation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{F}(s)$$\end{document}ZF(s) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C. Recently, Manickam in [16] showed that the first Fourier-Jacobi coefficient of a degree 2 cuspidal Siegel eigenform does not vanish identically, hence this is actually not a restriction.

It was not too long after the work of Kohnen and Skoruppa that Gritsenko in [8] considered a same type Dirichlet series, however this time involving the Fourier–Jacobi coefficients of two Hermitian cusp forms of degree 2. Gritsenko managed to prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_{F,G}(s)$$\end{document}DF,G(s) has an analytic continuation to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C and that if F is Hecke eigenform and G is in the corresponding Maass space, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_{F,G}(s)$$\end{document}DF,G(s) is related to a degree 6 L-function attached to F ([8, (5.3)]). This L-function was first introduced by Gritsenko in [6], in his study of a Dirichlet series involving the Fourier coefficients of a Hermitian cuspidal eigenform of degree 2. It is related to the exterior square representation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GL}_4$$\end{document}GL4 (see [19, Section 3.2.2] for example).

Even though the results in [13] and [8] are very similar in nature, the methods used by the authors are quite different. In particular, in [13], the authors prove their main result using a fundamental identity proved by Andrianov in [1]. This gives an Euler product expression for a certain Dirichlet series involving the Fourier coefficients of a Siegel cuspidal eigenform of degree 2. In [8] on the other hand, Gritsenko manages to factorise the polynomials defining the p-factors of the degree 6 L-function attached to a Hermitian cuspidal eigenform of degree 2. This factorisation happens in some parabolic Hecke rings which contain elements acting on the Fourier–Jacobi coefficients.

In this paper, we consider a Dirichlet series of the same type, attached to two cusp forms F and G for orthogonal groups of real signature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2,n+2), \ n \ge 1$$\end{document}(2,n+2),n≥1. We take F to be a Hecke eigenform for the corresponding Hecke algebra and G to be a Maass lift of a Poincaré series. The Maass space in this setting has been defined by Gritsenko in [7], Sugano in [28] and Krieg in [14]. Our main goal is to show how the method of Kohnen and Skoruppa in [13] can be extended in this setting and investigate the connection of this Dirichlet series with the standard L-function attached to F. The question of its analytic continuation and functional equation is quite different in nature and is the subject matter of our paper [20].

The main reason we expect such a connection is the accidental isogenies of orthogonal groups of signature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2,n+2)$$\end{document}(2,n+2) with classical groups for small n. For example, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1$$\end{document}n=1, there is an isogeny with symplectic groups of degree 2, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2$$\end{document}n=2, an isogeny with unitary groups of degree 2, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=4$$\end{document}n=4 with quaternionic groups of degree 2 and for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=6$$\end{document}n=6 with the linear groups of Cayley numbers of degree 2 (see for example [21, page 5]). Therefore, already the results of Kohnen and Skoruppa in [13] and Gritsenko in [8] indicate a connection in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1,2$$\end{document}n=1,2 case. We note here that the isogeny between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {SO}(2,3)$$\end{document}SO(2,3) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Sp}_2$$\end{document}Sp2 in particular was recently exploited by Pollack and Shah in [18] in order to extend the integral of Kohnen and Skoruppa to apply for arbitrary cuspidal automorphic representations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {PGSp}_2$$\end{document}PGSp2.

It should be noted that Gritsenko in [5] initiated the study of a Dirichlet series involving the Fourier coefficients of an orthogonal modular form and its connection with the standard L-function attached to it, following factorisation methods similar to [8]. Sugano in [27] extended Gritsenko’s result by proving an Euler product relation for a Dirichlet series involving twists of the Fourier coefficients by modular forms on a definite orthogonal group of lower rank. His work can be seen as an extension of Andrianov’s work in [1] in the orthogonal setting. As we mentioned, our methods in this paper can be considered as an extension of the methods employed by Kohnen and Skoruppa in [13], and therefore the result in [27] by Sugano is pivotal. Finally, results proved by Shimura in [24] on the sets of solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (x,x)=q$$\end{document}ϕ(x,x)=q, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi $$\end{document}ϕ is a bilinear form and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \in \mathbb {Q}^{\times }$$\end{document}q∈Q×, turn out to be crucial.

We remark that modified Dirichlet series involving Fourier-Jacobi coefficients can also be related to Euler products. This happens in [12] for example, where the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GSp}_2\times \text {GL}_2$$\end{document}GSp2×GL2 is treated. Jointly with Bouganis in [2], we partially extended this idea to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {GU}(2,2)\times \text {GL}_2$$\end{document}GU(2,2)×GL2. The motivation is that these Dirichlet series admit integral representations of Rankin-Selberg type and therefore provide a way of proving the analytic properties of L-functions but also results on the algebraicity of their special values (see for example [3], where the result in [12] finds a profound application). The isogenies we mentioned above mean that we can consider these as integral representations for the twisted standard L-functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {SO}(2,3)$$\end{document}SO(2,3) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {SO}(2,4)$$\end{document}SO(2,4) respectively.

Let us now give a brief outline of our results. The setup is as follows: We start with an even positive definite symmetric matrix S of rank \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \ge 1$$\end{document}n≥1 so that the lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L:=\mathbb {Z}^{n}$$\end{document}L:=Zn is maximal. We then set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_0 := \begin{pmatrix}& & 1\\ & -S& \\ 1& & \end{pmatrix}, \quad S_1 := \begin{pmatrix}& & 1\\ & S_0& \\ 1& & \end{pmatrix} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S0:=1-S1,S1:=1S01of real signature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1,n+1)$$\end{document}(1,n+1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2,n+2)$$\end{document}(2,n+2) respectively. If now K is a field containing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}Q, we define the corresponding special orthogonal groups of K-rational points via\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G^{*}_{K} = \{g \in \hbox {SL}_{n+2}(K) \mid g^{t}S_0g = S_0\}, \quad G_{K} = \{g \in \hbox {SL}_{n+4}(K) \mid g^{t}S_1g = S_1\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GK∗={g∈SLn+2(K)∣gtS0g=S0},GK={g∈SLn+4(K)∣gtS1g=S1}.The connected component of the identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathbb {R}}^{0}$$\end{document}GR0 acts on a suitable upper half plane, which we will call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{S} \subset \mathbb {C}^{n+2}$$\end{document}HS⊂Cn+2, and a suitable factor of automorphy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j(\gamma , Z)$$\end{document}j(γ,Z) can be defined. Let us now denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _{S}$$\end{document}ΓS the intersection of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathbb {R}}^{0}$$\end{document}GR0 with the stabiliser of the lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1 = \mathbb {Z}^{n+4}$$\end{document}L1=Zn+4 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F : \mathcal {H}_S \longrightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}F:HS⟶C be an orthogonal cusp form of integral even weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k>n/2+2$$\end{document}k>n/2+2 with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _{S}$$\end{document}ΓS (see Definitions 2.2 and 2.5). If now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z = (\tau ', z, \tau ) \in \mathcal {H}_{S}$$\end{document}Z=(τ′,z,τ)∈HS, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau , \tau ' \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}τ,τ′∈C and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z\in \mathbb {C}^{n+2}$$\end{document}z∈Cn+2, we can write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(Z) = \sum _{m \ge 1}\phi _m(\tau , z)e^{2\pi i m \tau '}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(Z)=∑m≥1ϕm(τ,z)e2πimτ′,and we call the functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _m(\tau , z)$$\end{document}ϕm(τ,z) the Fourier–Jacobi coefficients of F. Now, if G is another orthogonal cusp form with respect to some subgroup \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Gamma }_S \le \Gamma \le \Gamma _{S}$$\end{document}Γ~S≤Γ≤ΓS (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Gamma }_S$$\end{document}Γ~S here denotes the discriminant kernel), and with corresponding Fourier–Jacobi coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\psi _{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty }$$\end{document}{ψm}m=1∞, the object of interest is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}_{F,G}(s) = \sum _{m\ge 1}\langle \phi _{m},\psi _{m}\rangle m^{-s}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DF,G(s)=∑m≥1⟨ϕm,ψm⟩m-s,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \;,\; \rangle $$\end{document}⟨,⟩ is a suitable inner product defined on the space of Fourier–Jacobi forms of certain weight and (lattice) index (see Definition 3.5). This series converges absolutely for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Re}(s) > k+1$$\end{document}Re(s)>k+1 and represents a holomorphic function in this domain (see Lemma 6.1). Our aim is to find an Euler product expression for this Dirichlet series.

We will now present our main Theorem 8.2 in a simplified form. Let F be an eigenform for some appropriately defined Hecke algebras (see Theorem 8.2) and with Fourier–Jacobi expansion as above. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ denote the bilinear form induced by S, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(D,r) \in \widetilde{\text {supp}}(L, \sigma )$$\end{document}(D,r)∈supp~(L,σ) (see Definition 5.1) and define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {P}_{k,D,r}(\tau ', z, \tau ) := \sum _{N \ge 1}(V_{N}P_{k,D,r})(\tau ,z)e(N\tau '), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pk,D,r(τ′,z,τ):=∑N≥1(VNPk,D,r)(τ,z)e(Nτ′),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{k,D,r}$$\end{document}Pk,D,r is a specific Fourier–Jacobi form which reproduces the Fourier coefficients of any Fourier–Jacobi form, under the Petersson inner product (see Section 5). Also, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{N}$$\end{document}VN denotes the index raising operator of Section 4, acting on Fourier–Jacobi forms. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0 = \mathbb {Z}^{n+2}$$\end{document}L0=Zn+2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0^{*} = S_0^{-1}L_0$$\end{document}L0∗=S0-1L0. Assume now it exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \in V_0:=\mathbb {Q}^{n+2}$$\end{document}ξ∈V0:=Qn+2 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\xi ^{t}S_0x \mid x \in L_0\}=\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}{ξtS0x∣x∈L0}=Z and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0[\xi ] = -2D/q$$\end{document}S0[ξ]=-2D/q, where q denotes the level of S (see Definition 2.4) and further assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A(\xi ) \ne 0$$\end{document}A(ξ)≠0 for the Fourier coefficient of F. Consider then the algebraic subgroup of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathbb {Q}}^{*}$$\end{document}GQ∗, defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(\xi )_{\mathbb {Q}} = \left\{ g \in G_{\mathbb {Q}}^{*} \mid g\xi = \xi \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(ξ)Q=g∈GQ∗∣gξ=ξ.We consider the case when the class number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:= \#(H(\xi )_{\mathbb {Q}}\backslash H(\xi )_{\mathbb {A}}/(H(\xi )_{\mathbb {A}}\cap C)$$\end{document}h:=#(H(ξ)Q\H(ξ)A/(H(ξ)A∩C) equals 1, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C = \{x \in G_{\mathbb {A}}^{*} \mid xL_0=L_0\}$$\end{document}C={x∈GA∗∣xL0=L0}. Here, the subscript denotes the adelization of an algebraic linear group (see Section 7). Then, our main Theorem 8.2 reads:

Theorem 8.2

With the notation and assumptions as above, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}P denote a sufficiently large (specified) finite set of prime numbers. Define then the Dirichlet series\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}_{F,\mathcal {P}_{k,D,r},\mathcal {P}}(s) := \sum _{\begin{array}{c} N=1\\ (N,p)=1\forall p \in \mathcal {P} \end{array}}^{\infty } \langle \phi _N, V_NP_{k,D,r}\rangle N^{-s}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DF,Pk,D,r,P(s):=∑N=1(N,p)=1∀p∈P∞⟨ϕN,VNPk,D,r⟩N-s.We then have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\mathcal {P}}\left( \textbf{1};s-k+(n+3)/2\right) \mathcal {D}_{F,P_{k,D,r},\mathcal {P}}(s)$$\end{document}LP1;s-k+(n+3)/2DF,Pk,D,r,P(s) can be written as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A(\xi )L_{\mathcal {P}}\left( F;s-k+(n+2)/2\right) \zeta _{\xi ,\mathcal {P}}(s-k+n+1)\times {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll}1 & \text {if }n \text { odd} \\ \zeta _{\mathcal {P}}(2s-2k+n+2)^{-1} & \text {if } n \text { even} \end{array}\right. }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A(ξ)LPF;s-k+(n+2)/2ζξ,P(s-k+n+1)×1ifnoddζP(2s-2k+n+2)-1ifneven.Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(-,s)$$\end{document}L(-,s) denotes the standard L-function attached to orthogonal modular forms and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{1}$$\end{document}1 denotes the constant function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(\xi )_{\mathbb {A}}$$\end{document}H(ξ)A. Also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{\xi }(s)$$\end{document}ζξ(s) is a Dirichlet series counting the number of solutions of congruences (see Proposition 7.3). For any zeta function (or Dirichlet series) appearing, we use the subscript \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}P to denote that we don’t take into account the p-factors (or p-terms) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in \mathcal {P}$$\end{document}p∈P.

Note that in Theorem 8.2, we cover the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h > 1$$\end{document}h>1 as well. The rest of the paper (Section 9) is devoted in obtaining explicit examples where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=1$$\end{document}h=1. In those cases, we obtain a clear-cut Euler product expression for the specific Dirichlet series \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_{F,\mathcal {P}_{k,-q,r}, \mathcal {P}}(s)$$\end{document}DF,Pk,-q,r,P(s) (here we make a particular choice for D, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D=-q$$\end{document}D=-q and take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi =(1,{\textbf {0}}, 1)^{t}$$\end{document}ξ=(1,0,1)t).

We find all instances when this happens in the rank 1 case and we give the result in Theorem 9.2. Note that in Theorem 9.2, we recover the case of Kohnen and Skoruppa in [13] for our choice of D (we take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=2$$\end{document}S=2), up to a finite number of Euler factors. What is more, we already produce new results related to L-functions attached to paramodular forms, as it is not clear how can one use the methods in [13] to produce such results for paramodular forms directly. Finally, in Theorem 9.3, we provide at least one explicit example for each of the cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2,4,6,8$$\end{document}n=2,4,6,8. These examples can be related to modular forms for other classical groups, such as Hermitian (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2$$\end{document}n=2) and quaternionic (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=4$$\end{document}n=4).

The structure of the paper is as follows: In Sections 2 and 3 we give the definitions of the main objects of consideration, i.e. orthogonal groups, modular forms and Fourier–Jacobi forms. In Section 4, we calculate the action of the adjoint of the level-raising operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN with respect to the Petersson inner product of Fourier–Jacobi forms. In Section 5, we define Poincaré series and in Section 6, we define the Dirichlet series of interest and prove some preliminary results. Section 7 is devoted in showing how results of Shimura in [24] and [26] can be used in our setting and in Section 8, we use Sugano’s theorem in [27] to deduce our main Theorem 8.2. Finally, in Section 9, we obtain clear-cut Euler product expressions for some specific orthogonal groups.

Preliminaries

Let V denote a finite dimensional vector space over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}Q. We start with the following definition.

Definition 2.1

A \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}Z-lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ is a free, finitely generated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}Z-module, which spans V over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}Q.
