\usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \in \text {Mat}(n+2, \mathbb {R}), \alpha ,\beta ,\gamma ,\delta \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}A∈Mat(n+2,R),α,β,γ,δ∈R and a, b, c, d real column vectors. Now the map1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Z \longmapsto g\langle Z\rangle = \frac{-\frac{1}{2}S_0[Z]b+AZ+c}{-\frac{1}{2}S_0[Z]\gamma +d^tZ+\delta } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Z⟼g⟨Z⟩=-12S0[Z]b+AZ+c-12S0[Z]γ+dtZ+δgives a well-defined transitive action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathbb {R}}^{0}$$\end{document}GR0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H_{S}}$$\end{document}HS, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathbb {R}}^{0}$$\end{document}GR0 denotes the identity component of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}GR. The denominator of the above expression is the factor of automorphy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} j(g, Z) := -\frac{1}{2}S_0[Z]\gamma +d^tZ+\delta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}j(g,Z):=-12S0[Z]γ+dtZ+δ.Let now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1 := \mathbb {Z}^{n+4}$$\end{document}L1:=Zn+4 and define the groups\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma (L_0):=\{g \in G_{\mathbb {Q}}^{*} \mid gL_0=L_0\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ(L0):={g∈GQ∗∣gL0=L0},\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma (L_1) := \{g \in G_{\mathbb {Q}} \mid gL_1 = L_1\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ(L1):={g∈GQ∣gL1=L1}.Let also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma ^{+}(L_0) = \Gamma (L_0) \cap G^{*,0}_{\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}Γ+(L0)=Γ(L0)∩GR∗,0. Let now\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma _{S} := G_{\mathbb {R}}^{0} \cap \Gamma (L_1), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΓS:=GR0∩Γ(L1),and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\Gamma }_S:= \{M \in \Gamma _S \mid M \in 1_{n+4} + \text {M}_{n+4}(\mathbb {Z})S_1\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ~S:={M∈ΓS∣M∈1n+4+Mn+4(Z)S1},the discriminant kernel.

Definition 2.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}k∈Z and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Gamma }_S\le \Gamma \le \Gamma _S$$\end{document}Γ~S≤Γ≤ΓS a subgroup of finite index. A holomorphic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F : \mathcal {H}_S \longrightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}F:HS⟶C is called a modular form of weight k with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ if it satisfies the equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (F|_k \gamma )(Z) := j(\gamma , Z)^{-k}F(\gamma \langle Z\rangle ) = F(Z) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(F|kγ)(Z):=j(γ,Z)-kF(γ⟨Z⟩)=F(Z)for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \Gamma $$\end{document}γ∈Γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z \in \mathcal {H}_S$$\end{document}Z∈HS. We will denote the set of such forms by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{k}(\Gamma )$$\end{document}Mk(Γ).

Let us now give a couple more definitions on lattices.

Definition 2.3

Given a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}Z-lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ, equipped with a bilinear form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma : V \times V \longrightarrow \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}σ:V×V⟶Q, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V = \Lambda \otimes _{\mathbb {Z}} \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}V=Λ⊗ZQ, we define its dual lattice by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Lambda ^{*} := \{x \in \Lambda \otimes _{\mathbb {Z}} \mathbb {Q} \mid \sigma (x, y) \in \mathbb {Z} \quad \forall y \in \Lambda \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Λ∗:={x∈Λ⊗ZQ∣σ(x,y)∈Z∀y∈Λ}.

Definition 2.4

With the notation as above, the level of the lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ is given as the least positive integer q such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}q\sigma (x,x) \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}12qσ(x,x)∈Z for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \Lambda ^{*}$$\end{document}x∈Λ∗.

Now, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Gamma }_S\le \Gamma \le \Gamma _S$$\end{document}Γ~S≤Γ≤ΓS, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in M_{k}(\Gamma )$$\end{document}F∈Mk(Γ) admits a Fourier expansion of the form (see [28, (5.10)])2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(Z) = \sum _{r \in L_0^{*}}A(r)e(r^{t}S_0Z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(Z)=∑r∈L0∗A(r)e(rtS0Z),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z \in \mathcal {H}_S$$\end{document}Z∈HS. It is then Koecher’s principle that gives us that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A(r)=0$$\end{document}A(r)=0 unless \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r \in L_0^{*} \cap \overline{\mathcal {P_S}}$$\end{document}r∈L0∗∩PS¯ (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathcal {P}_S}$$\end{document}PS¯ denotes the closure of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P_S}$$\end{document}PS, see [21, Theorem 1.5.2]). By [21, Theorem 1.6.23], and because L is maximal, we have the following definition for cusp forms.

Definition 2.5

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Gamma }_S\le \Gamma \le \Gamma _S$$\end{document}Γ~S≤Γ≤ΓS, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in M_{k}(\Gamma )$$\end{document}F∈Mk(Γ) is called a cusp form if it admits a Fourier expansion of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(Z) = \sum _{r \in L_0^{*} \cap \mathcal {P_S}}A(r)e(r^{t}S_0Z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(Z)=∑r∈L0∗∩PSA(r)e(rtS0Z),We denote the space of cusp forms by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_k(\Gamma )$$\end{document}Sk(Γ).

Finally, we give the definition of the Maass space, due to Krieg in [14, Section 5].

Definition 2.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Gamma }_S\le \Gamma \le \Gamma _S$$\end{document}Γ~S≤Γ≤ΓS. The Maass space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{k}^{*}(\Gamma )$$\end{document}Mk∗(Γ) consists of all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in M_{k}(\Gamma )$$\end{document}F∈Mk(Γ), so that if their Fourier expansion is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(Z) = \sum _{r \in L_0^{*}}A(r)e(r^{t}S_0Z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(Z)=∑r∈L0∗A(r)e(rtS0Z),we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A(r) = \sum _{d \mid \gcd (\rho )} d^{k-1}A\left( \begin{pmatrix}lm/d^2\S ^{-1}\lambda /d\\ 1\end{pmatrix}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A(r)=∑d∣gcd(ρ)dk-1Alm/d2§-1λ/d1,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r = S_0^{-1}\rho $$\end{document}r=S0-1ρ, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho = \begin{pmatrix}m&\lambda&l\end{pmatrix}^{t}$$\end{document}ρ=mλlt. We also write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_k^{*}(\Gamma )$$\end{document}Sk∗(Γ) for the Maass space consisting of cusp forms.

Fourier–Jacobi Forms

In this section, we will define Fourier–Jacobi forms of lattice index. We mainly follow Mocanu’s thesis in [17] and Krieg in [14].

For now, assume that V is a vector space of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n<\infty $$\end{document}n<∞ over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}Q together with a positive definite symmetric bilinear form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ and an even lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ in V, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (\lambda , \lambda ) \in 2\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}σ(λ,λ)∈2Z for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \Lambda $$\end{document}λ∈Λ. We start with the following definitions:

Definition 3.1

We define the Heisenberg group to be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{(\Lambda ,\sigma )}(\mathbb {R}) = \{(x,y,\zeta ) \mid x,y \in \Lambda \otimes \mathbb {R}, \zeta \in S^{1}\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(Λ,σ)(R)={(x,y,ζ)∣x,y∈Λ⊗R,ζ∈S1},where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^{1}:=\{z \in \mathbb {C} \mid |z|=1\}$$\end{document}S1:={z∈C∣|z|=1}, equipped with the following composition law:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (x_1,y_1,\zeta _1)(x_2,y_2,\zeta _2) := (x_1+x_2, y_1+y_2, \zeta _1\zeta _2e(\sigma (x_1,y_2))). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(x1,y1,ζ1)(x2,y2,ζ2):=(x1+x2,y1+y2,ζ1ζ2e(σ(x1,y2))).The integral Heisenberg group is defined to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(\Lambda ,\sigma )}(\mathbb {Z}):= \{(x,y,1) \mid x,y \in \Lambda \}$$\end{document}H(Λ,σ)(Z):={(x,y,1)∣x,y∈Λ} and in the following we drop the last coordinate for convenience.

Proposition 3.1

The group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {SL}_2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}SL2(R) acts on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}H(Λ,σ)(R) from the right, via\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ((x,y,\zeta ), A) \longmapsto (x,y,\zeta )^{A} := \left( (x,y)A, \zeta e\left( \sigma [(x,y)A]-\frac{1}{2}\sigma (x,y)\right) \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}((x,y,ζ),A)⟼(x,y,ζ)A:=(x,y)A,ζeσ[(x,y)A]-12σ(x,y).where (x, y)A denotes the formal multiplication of the vector (x, y) with A, i.e. if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A =\begin{pmatrix}a& b\\ c& d\end{pmatrix}$$\end{document}A=abcd, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,y)A := (ax+cy, bx+dy)$$\end{document}(x,y)A:=(ax+cy,bx+dy).

Definition 3.2

The real Jacobi group associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Lambda , \sigma )$$\end{document}(Λ,σ), denoted with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}J(Λ,σ)(R), is defined to be the semi-direct product of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {SL}_2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}SL2(R) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}H(Λ,σ)(R). The composition law is then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (A,h)\cdot (A',h') := (AA', h^{A'}h'). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(A,h)·(A′,h′):=(AA′,h′h′).We also define the integral Jacobi group to be the semi-direct product of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {SL}_{2}(\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL2(Z) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}(\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}H(Λ,σ)(Z) and we will denote it by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}$$\end{document}J(Λ,σ).

We are now going to define some slash operators, acting on holomorphic, complex-valued functions on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H} \times (\Lambda \otimes \mathbb {C})$$\end{document}H×(Λ⊗C).

Definition 3.3

Let k be a positive integer and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f : \mathbb {H} \times (\Lambda \otimes \mathbb {C}) \longrightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}f:H×(Λ⊗C)⟶C a holomorphic function. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M =\begin{pmatrix}a& b\\ c& d\end{pmatrix} \in \hbox {SL}_{2}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}M=abcd∈SL2(R) , we define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (f|_{k, (\Lambda , \sigma )} [M])(\tau , z) := (c\tau +d)^{-k}e^{-\pi i c\sigma (z,z)/(c\tau + d)}f\left( \frac{a\tau +b}{c\tau +d}, \frac{z}{c\tau +d}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(f|k,(Λ,σ)[M])(τ,z):=(cτ+d)-ke-πicσ(z,z)/(cτ+d)faτ+bcτ+d,zcτ+d.In the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \in \hbox {GL}_2^{+}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}M∈GL2+(R), we define the action by using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det (M)^{-1/2}M$$\end{document}det(M)-1/2M instead of M. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h = (x,y,\zeta ) \in H^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}h=(x,y,ζ)∈H(Λ,σ)(R), we define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (f \mid _{k, (\Lambda ,\sigma )}h)(\tau ,z): = \zeta \cdot e^{\pi i \tau \sigma (x, x)+2\pi i \sigma (x, z)}f(\tau , z+x\tau +y). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(f∣k,(Λ,σ)h)(τ,z):=ζ·eπiτσ(x,x)+2πiσ(x,z)f(τ,z+xτ+y).Finally, for the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}J(Λ,σ)(R), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (f, (A,h)) \longmapsto (f \mid _{k, (\Lambda , \sigma )}(A,h))(\tau , z) := (f \mid _{k, (\Lambda , \sigma )} A) \mid _{k, (\Lambda , \sigma )} h. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(f,(A,h))⟼(f∣k,(Λ,σ)(A,h))(τ,z):=(f∣k,(Λ,σ)A)∣k,(Λ,σ)h.

We now have the following definition ([17, Definition 1.23]).

Definition 3.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\mathbb {C}} := V \otimes \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}VC:=V⊗C and extend \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}VC by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C-linearity. For k a positive integer, a holomorphic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: \mathbb {H} \times V_{\mathbb {C}} \rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}f:H×VC→C (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}$$\end{document}H denotes the usual upper half plane) is called a Fourier–Jacobi form of weight k with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Lambda , \sigma )$$\end{document}(Λ,σ) if the following hold:For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in J^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}$$\end{document}γ∈J(Λ,σ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau , z) \in \mathbb {H} \times V_{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}(τ,z)∈H×VC, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (f \mid _{k, (\Lambda , \sigma )} \gamma ) (\tau ,z) = f(\tau ,z). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(f∣k,(Λ,σ)γ)(τ,z)=f(τ,z).EMPTY f has a Fourier expansion of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\tau , z) = \sum _{m \in \mathbb {Z}, r \in \Lambda ^{*}, 2m \ge \sigma [r]} c_{f}(m,r)e(m\tau + \sigma (r, z)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(τ,z)=∑m∈Z,r∈Λ∗,2m≥σ[r]cf(m,r)e(mτ+σ(r,z)).We denote the space of such forms by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{k}(\Lambda , \sigma )$$\end{document}Jk(Λ,σ). We say f is a Jacobi cusp form if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_f(m,r)=0$$\end{document}cf(m,r)=0 when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2m= \sigma [r]$$\end{document}2m=σ[r]. We denote the space of Fourier–Jacobi cusp forms by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{k}(\Lambda , \sigma )$$\end{document}Sk(Λ,σ).

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in J^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}$$\end{document}γ∈J(Λ,σ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau , z) \in \mathbb {H} \times V_{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}(τ,z)∈H×VC, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (f \mid _{k, (\Lambda , \sigma )} \gamma ) (\tau ,z) = f(\tau ,z). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(f∣k,(Λ,σ)γ)(τ,z)=f(τ,z).

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in J^{(\Lambda , \sigma )}$$\end{document}γ∈J(Λ,σ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau , z) \in \mathbb {H} \times V_{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}(τ,z)∈H×VC, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (f \mid _{k, (\Lambda , \sigma )} \gamma ) (\tau ,z) = f(\tau ,z). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(f∣k,(Λ,σ)γ)(τ,z)=f(τ,z).

EMPTY f has a Fourier expansion of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\tau , z) = \sum _{m \in \mathbb {Z}, r \in \Lambda ^{*}, 2m \ge \sigma [r]} c_{f}(m,r)e(m\tau + \sigma (r, z)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(τ,z)=∑m∈Z,r∈Λ∗,2m≥σ[r]cf(m,r)e(mτ+σ(r,z)).

EMPTY f has a Fourier expansion of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\tau , z) = \sum _{m \in \mathbb {Z}, r \in \Lambda ^{*}, 2m \ge \sigma [r]} c_{f}(m,r)e(m\tau + \sigma (r, z)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(τ,z)=∑m∈Z,r∈Λ∗,2m≥σ[r]cf(m,r)e(mτ+σ(r,z)).

Remark 1

We could also characterise Fourier–Jacobi forms as modular forms transforming under the action of the parabolic subgroup of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _{S}$$\end{document}ΓS, given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma _{S,J} := \left\{ \begin{pmatrix}*& *\\ 0& D\end{pmatrix} \in \Gamma _{S} \mid D \in \text {SL}_{2}(\mathbb {Z})\right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΓS,J:=∗∗0D∈ΓS∣D∈SL2(Z).This is true because there is an embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota : J_S \longrightarrow \Gamma _{S,J}$$\end{document}ι:JS⟶ΓS,J (see [21, p. 44] or [28, p. 27]).

We now have a notion of a scalar product for elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{k}(\Lambda , \sigma )$$\end{document}Sk(Λ,σ) ([17, Definition 1.33]).

Definition 3.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi , \psi \in S_{k}(\Lambda , \sigma )$$\end{document}ϕ,ψ∈Sk(Λ,σ). If then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \le J^{(\Lambda ,\sigma )}$$\end{document}U≤J(Λ,σ) of finite index, we define their Petersson inner product via\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \phi , \psi \rangle _{U} = \frac{1}{\left[ J^{\Lambda }:U\right] }\int _{U \backslash \mathcal {H}\times (\Lambda \otimes \mathbb {C})} \phi (\tau ,z)\overline{\psi (\tau , z)}v^{k}e^{-2\pi \sigma (y,y)v^{-1}}\text {d}V, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ϕ,ψ⟩U=1JΛ:U∫U\H×(Λ⊗C)ϕ(τ,z)ψ(τ,z)¯vke-2πσ(y,y)v-1dV,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau = u+iv$$\end{document}τ=u+iv, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z = x+iy$$\end{document}z=x+iy and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {d}V := v^{-n-2}\text {d}u\text {d}v\text {d}x\text {d}y$$\end{document}dV:=v-n-2dudvdxdy. This inner product does not depend on the choice of U, so in what follows, we drop the subscript.

We now specify to our case, by taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda = L = \mathbb {Z}^{n}$$\end{document}Λ=L=Zn and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (x,y) = x^{t}Sy$$\end{document}σ(x,y)=xtSy for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,y \in V$$\end{document}x,y∈V.

Let us discuss the Fourier–Jacobi expansion of orthogonal cusp forms of weight k with respect to a subgroup \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Gamma }_{S} \le \Gamma \le \Gamma _S$$\end{document}Γ~S≤Γ≤ΓS. If we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z = (\tau ', z, \tau ) \in \mathcal {H}_{S}$$\end{document}Z=(τ′,z,τ)∈HS with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ',\tau \in \mathbb {C}, \ z \in \mathbb {C}^{n}$$\end{document}τ′,τ∈C,z∈Cn, we have that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in S_k(\Gamma )$$\end{document}F∈Sk(Γ) and any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}m∈Z:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(\tau '+m, z, \tau ) = F(\tau ', z, \tau ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(τ′+m,z,τ)=F(τ′,z,τ).Hence, we can write3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(Z) = \sum _{m \ge 1}\phi _m(\tau , z)e^{2\pi i m \tau '}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(Z)=∑m≥1ϕm(τ,z)e2πimτ′,and we call the functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _m(\tau , z)$$\end{document}ϕm(τ,z) the Fourier–Jacobi coefficients of F. We note that then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _{m} \in S_{k}(\mathbb {Z}^{n}, m\sigma )$$\end{document}ϕm∈Sk(Zn,mσ) (see [28, (5.10), (5.11)]).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{N}$$\end{document}VN operator

Let now again \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (x,y) = x^{t}Sy$$\end{document}σ(x,y)=xtSy for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,y \in V$$\end{document}x,y∈V. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \ge 1$$\end{document}N≥1 and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{2}(\mathbb {Z})_N := \{g \in M_{2}(\mathbb {Z}) | \det g = N\}$$\end{document}M2(Z)N:={g∈M2(Z)|detg=N}. For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M = \begin{pmatrix}a& b\\ c& d\end{pmatrix} \in M_{2}(\mathbb {Z})_N$$\end{document}M=abcd∈M2(Z)N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in \mathbb {H}$$\end{document}τ∈H (usual upper half plane), we define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M\langle \tau \rangle := \frac{a\tau +b}{c\tau +d}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M⟨τ⟩:=aτ+bcτ+d.Given a Fourier–Jacobi cusp form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi \in S_k(\mathbb {Z}^{n}, \sigma )$$\end{document}ϕ∈Sk(Zn,σ), we define the operator\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_{N} : S_{k}(\mathbb {Z}^{n}, \sigma ) \longrightarrow S_{k}(\mathbb {Z}^{n}, N\sigma ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VN:Sk(Zn,σ)⟶Sk(Zn,Nσ),given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (V_{N}\phi )(\tau , z) = N^{k-1}\sum _{M \in \text {SL}_{2}(\mathbb {Z})\backslash M_{2}(\mathbb {Z})_{N}} (c\tau +d)^{-k}e^{-\pi i cNS[z]/(c\tau +d)}\phi \left( M\langle \tau \rangle , \frac{Nz}{c\tau +d}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(VNϕ)(τ,z)=Nk-1∑M∈SL2(Z)\M2(Z)N(cτ+d)-ke-πicNS[z]/(cτ+d)ϕM⟨τ⟩,Nzcτ+d,This is well-defined by [28, Lemma 6.1] or [17, Definition 4.25]. Our aim in this Section is to compute its adjoint with respect to the scalar product of Fourier–Jacobi forms. This is the analogue of the main Proposition in [13].

Now, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi \in S_k(\mathbb {Z}^{n}, N\sigma )$$\end{document}ϕ∈Sk(Zn,Nσ), we will write its Fourier expansion in a form similar to Kohnen and Skoruppa in [13, Section 2]. From Definition 3.4, we can write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi (\tau , z) = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} m \in \mathbb {Z}, r \in \mathbb {Z}^{n}\\ 2Nm > r^{t}S^{-1}r \end{array}}c_{\phi }(m,r)e(m\tau + r^{t}z), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ(τ,z)=∑m∈Z,r∈Zn2Nm>rtS-1rcϕ(m,r)e(mτ+rtz),for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{\phi }(m,r) \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}cϕ(m,r)∈C. Now, from the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2Nm > r^{t}S^{-1}r$$\end{document}2Nm>rtS-1r and the definition of the level (see Definition 2.4), we can write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Nmq - \frac{1}{2}qS^{-1}[r] = -D \implies m = \frac{\frac{1}{2}qS^{-1}[r]-D}{qN}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Nmq-12qS-1[r]=-D⇒m=12qS-1[r]-DqN,for some integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D < 0$$\end{document}D<0. Therefore, we can write4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi (\tau , z) = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} D \in \mathbb {Z}_{<0}, r \in \mathbb {Z}^{n}\\ D \equiv \frac{1}{2}qS^{-1}[r] \mod qN \end{array}}c_{\phi }\left( D, r\right) e\left( \frac{\frac{1}{2}qS^{-1}[r]-D}{qN}\tau + r^{t} z\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ(τ,z)=∑D∈Z<0,r∈ZnD≡12qS-1[r]modqNcϕD,re12qS-1[r]-DqNτ+rtz.

Remark 2

By adjusting [17, Proposition 1.25] to the notation above, we have that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D=D'\text { and } S^{-1}r \equiv S^{-1}r' \pmod {N \mathbb {Z}^{n}} \implies c_{\phi }(D,r) = c_{\phi }(D',r') . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}D=D′andS-1r≡S-1r′(modNZn)⇒cϕ(D,r)=cϕ(D′,r′).

We are now ready to give the result concerning the adjoint of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN. We use the notation that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \equiv s' \ (\textrm{mod}\ NS\mathbb {Z}^{n}) \iff s-s' = NSu$$\end{document}s≡s′(modNSZn)⟺s-s′=NSu for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \mathbb {Z}^n$$\end{document}u∈Zn.

Proposition 4.1

The action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Remark 3

The (D, r) coefficient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{N}^{*}\phi $$\end{document}VN∗ϕ is independent of r.

We start by writing\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_{N}\phi = N^{k/2-1} \sum _{A \in \text {SL}_2(\mathbb {Z}) \backslash M_{2}(\mathbb {Z})_{N}}\phi _{\sqrt{N}} |_{k,(\mathbb {Z}^{n},N\sigma )} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}A\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VNϕ=Nk/2-1∑A∈SL2(Z)\M2(Z)NϕN|k,(Zn,Nσ)1NA,where we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _{\sqrt{N}}(\tau , z) := \phi \left( \tau , \sqrt{N}z\right) $$\end{document}ϕN(τ,z):=ϕτ,Nz.
